<div class="ra xbox p" role="complementary" aria-label="Related or updated information about this article"><div>The publisher's version of this article is available at <a href="https://doi.org/10.1029/2018JD028491" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">J Geophys Res Atmos</a>
</div></div>
</div></section></section><section aria-label="Article content"><section class="body main-article-body"><section class="abstract" id="ABS1"><h2>Abstract</h2>
<p id="P1">During the 2013 Southern Oxidant and Aerosol Study, Fourier Transform Infrared Spectroscopy (FTIR) and Aerosol Mass Spectrometer (AMS) measurements of submicron mass were collected at Look Rock (LRK), Tennessee, and Centreville (CTR), Alabama. Carbon monoxide and submicron sulfate and organic mass concentrations were 15–60% higher at CTR than at LRK but their time series had moderate correlations (r~0.5). However, NO<sub>x</sub> had no correlation (r=0.08) between the two sites with nighttime-to-early-morning peaks 3~10 times higher at CTR than at LRK. Organic mass (OM) sources identified by FTIR Positive Matrix Factorization (PMF) had three very similar factors at both sites: Fossil Fuel Combustion (FFC) related organic aerosols, Mixed Organic Aerosols (MOA), and Biogenic Organic Aerosols (BOA). The BOA spectrum from FTIR is similar (cosine similarity &gt; 0.6) to that of lab-generated particle mass from the photochemical oxidation of both isoprene and monoterpenes under high NO<sub>x</sub> conditions from chamber experiments. The BOA mass fraction was highest during the night at CTR but in the afternoon at LRK. AMS PMF resulted in two similar pairs of factors at both sites and a third nighttime NO<sub>x</sub>-related factor (33% of OM) at CTR but a daytime nitrate-related factor (28% of OM) at LRK. NO<sub>x</sub> was correlated with BOA and LO-OOA for NO<sub>x</sub> concentrations higher than 1 ppb at both sites, producing 0.5 ± 0.1 μg m<sup>−3</sup> for CTR-LO-OOA and 1.0 ± 0.3 μg m<sup>−3</sup> for CTR-BOA above 1 ppb additional biogenic OM for each 1 ppb increase of NO<sub>x</sub>.</p>
<section id="kwd-group1" class="kwd-group"><p><strong>Keywords:</strong> Biogenic Organic Aerosol, Positive Matrix Factorization, Aerosol Mass Spectrometer, Fourier Transform Infrared Spectroscopy, NO<sub>x</sub></p></section></section><section id="S1"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">1. Introduction</h2>
<p id="P2">Biogenic secondary organic aerosols (SOA) are estimated to be increased by as much as 78% because of interactions involving anthropogenic emissions on the U.S. east coast [<a href="#R12" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R12">Carlton et al., 2010</a>] and in eastern Asia [<a href="#R46" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R46">Matsui et al., 2014</a>]. The resulting aerosol particle mass may account for as much as 70% of the global organic aerosol budget [<a href="#R76" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R76">Spracklen et al., 2011</a>]. More than 90% of sulfate (from SO<sub>2</sub>) and nitrogen oxides (NO<sub>x</sub>, or NO+NO<sub>2</sub>) are anthropogenic and increase the yield of biogenic SOA [<a href="#R73" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R73">Shilling et al., 2013</a>; <a href="#R74" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R74">Shrivastava et al., 2017</a>; <a href="#R76" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R76">Spracklen et al., 2011</a>; <a href="#R89" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R89">Xu et al., 2015a</a>]. For example, some field studies have shown that a 1 μg m<sup>−3</sup> decrease in sulfate can lead to a 0.2–0.42 μg m<sup>−3</sup> decrease in isoprene SOA [<a href="#R7" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R7">Blanchard et al., 2016</a>; <a href="#R11" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R11">Budisulistiorini et al., 2017</a>; <a href="#R63" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R63">Pye et al., 2013</a>; <a href="#R74" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R74">Shrivastava et al., 2017</a>; <a href="#R89" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R89">Xu et al., 2015a</a>; <a href="#R91" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R91">Xu et al., 2016</a>]. Similarly, decreases in NO<sub>x</sub> have been shown to decrease biogenic SOA formation but there are also studies that have shown increases of biogenic SOA in some regimes with decreased NO<sub>x</sub> (<a href="#T1" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>) [<a href="#R19" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R19">de Sa et al., 2017</a>; <a href="#R22" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R22">Edwards et al., 2017</a>; <a href="#R33" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R33">Kroll et al., 2006</a>; <a href="#R35" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R35">Lane et al., 2008</a>; <a href="#R41" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R41">Liu et al., 2016</a>; <a href="#R46" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R46">Matsui et al., 2014</a>; <a href="#R53" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R53">Ng et al., 2007</a>; <a href="#R60" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R60">Pye et al., 2010</a>; <a href="#R61" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R61">Pye et al., 2015</a>; <a href="#R63" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R63">Pye et al., 2013</a>; <a href="#R65" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R65">Rollins et al., 2012</a>; <a href="#R87" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R87">Wildt et al., 2014</a>; <a href="#R89" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R89">Xu et al., 2015a</a>; <a href="#R90" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R90">Xu et al., 2014</a>; <a href="#R96" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R96">Zhang et al., 2017</a>; <a href="#R97" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R97">Zheng et al., 2015</a>]. Since these effects of NO<sub>x</sub> have been shown by laboratory studies to affect biogenic SOA formation by changing oxidation pathways and ultimate products [<a href="#R5" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R5">Atkinson et al., 2004</a>; <a href="#R29" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R29">Hoyle et al., 2011</a>; <a href="#R33" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R33">Kroll et al., 2006</a>; <a href="#R59" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R59">Presto et al., 2005</a>; <a href="#R74" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R74">Shrivastava et al., 2017</a>; <a href="#R79" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R79">Surratt et al., 2006</a>; <a href="#R98" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R98">Ziemann and Atkinson, 2012</a>], it is important to quantify them in atmospheric field studies.</p>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/T1/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section><p id="P3">Chemical transport models capture some aspects of the influence of NO<sub>x</sub> on organic aerosols. <a href="#R97" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R97">Zheng et al. [2015]</a> used an updated SOA scheme in the global NCAR (National Center for Atmospheric Research) Community Atmospheric Model version 4 with chemistry (CAM4-chem) with a 4-product volatility basis set scheme with NO<sub>x</sub>-dependent SOA yields and aging parameterizations and predicted only 6–12% biogenic SOA decreases in the southeastern U.S. for 50% NO<sub>x</sub> reductions. <a href="#R61" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R61">Pye et al. [2015]</a> updated the comprehensive coupled gas and aerosol processes in CMAQ5.1 with SAPRC07tic (State Air Pollution Research Center mechanism update, <a href="https://www.airqualitymodeling.org/index.php/CMAQ_v5.1_SAPRC07tic_AE6i" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://www.airqualitymodeling.org/index.php/CMAQ_v5.1_SAPRC07tic_AE6i</a>) [<a href="#R31" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R31">Hutzell et al., 2012</a>; <a href="#R38" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R38">Lin et al., 2013</a>; <a href="#R88" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R88">Xie et al., 2013</a>]. NO<sub>3</sub>–related reactions of monoterpenes (MTNO3) and isoprene as well as monoterpene peroxy radical reactions with NO resulted in semivolatile organic nitrates that contributed to organic aerosol [<a href="#R61" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R61">Pye et al., 2015</a>]. The model predicted a 25% reduction in NO<sub>x</sub> emissions would cause a 9% reduction in organic aerosol for June 2013 in Centreville, Alabama.</p>
<p id="P4">These model schemes incorporate the results of laboratory experiments that show that NO<sub>x</sub> affects biogenic SOA formation in at least three different, competing and counteracting ways. First, NO<sub>x</sub> can reduce O<sub>3</sub> formation in the high-NO<sub>x</sub> regime but under low-NO<sub>x</sub> conditions increasing NO<sub>x</sub> will result in increases in OH radicals and O<sub>3</sub> [<a href="#R71" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R71">Seinfeld and Pandis, 2016</a>], both of which result in higher SOA yields [<a href="#R97" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R97">Zheng et al., 2015</a>]. Second, high concentrations of nighttime nitrate radical (NO<sub>3</sub>) increased SOA formation from isoprene in chamber experiments [<a href="#R56" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R56">Ng et al., 2008</a>] by forming organonitrates [<a href="#R54" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R54">Ng et al., 2017</a>]. Third, reaction of NO with organo-peroxy radicals (RO<sub>2</sub>) in the high NO<sub>x</sub> regime can lower SOA yields due to more volatile products compared to reaction with hydroperoxy radicals (HO<sub>2</sub>) [<a href="#R33" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R33">Kroll et al., 2006</a>; <a href="#R34" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R34">Kroll and Seinfeld, 2008</a>; <a href="#R97" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R97">Zheng et al., 2015</a>; <a href="#R98" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R98">Ziemann and Atkinson, 2012</a>]. However, the role of NO<sub>x</sub> in systems that undergo autoxidation, particularly monoterpenes [<a href="#R23" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R23">Ehn et al., 2014</a>], has not been elucidated. These multi-faceted effects mean that the role of NO<sub>x</sub> on biogenic SOA formation in the atmosphere depends on the specific atmospheric conditions and precursors that are present.</p>
<p id="P5">In order to use field measurements as constraints for models, we need sufficient aerosol chemical composition measurements to separate biogenic SOA from other organic aerosol components. The separation is difficult because biogenic SOA formation is influenced by the same oxidants that form other SOA, and there is often overlap between primary and secondary emission sources. Positive Matrix Factorization (PMF) of AMS measurements [<a href="#R36" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R36">Lanz et al., 2007</a>; <a href="#R84" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R84">Ulbrich et al., 2009</a>] use high time resolution to separate small differences in the timing of emissions and photochemical accumulation [<a href="#R16" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R16">Corrigan et al., 2013</a>; <a href="#R39" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R39">Liu et al., 2012</a>], but FTIR [<a href="#R27" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R27">Hallquist et al., 2009</a>; <a href="#R66" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R66">Russell, 2014</a>; <a href="#R67" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R67">Russell et al., 2009</a>] provides specific chemical fingerprints that associate co-emitted primary and secondary components [<a href="#R68" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R68">Russell et al., 2011</a>]. For example, PMF of FTIR has provided substantial evidence for non-acid carbonyl groups associated with biogenic SOA in forest conditions [<a href="#R16" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R16">Corrigan et al., 2013</a>; <a href="#R70" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R70">Schwartz et al., 2010</a>; <a href="#R81" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R81">Takahama et al., 2011</a>]. One challenge is that a common AMS PMF factor has highly oxidized organic fragments with high <em>m/z</em> 28 and <em>m/z</em> 44 and accounts for a substantial fraction of OM both from anthropogenic sources like vehicle emissions [<a href="#R58" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R58">Presto et al., 2014</a>] and from natural biogenic emissions [<a href="#R14" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R14">Chen et al., 2015</a>; <a href="#R89" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R89">Xu et al., 2015a</a>]. In addition, oxidized organic fragments can also result from vegetative detritus [<a href="#R16" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R16">Corrigan et al., 2013</a>; <a href="#R81" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R81">Takahama et al., 2011</a>], which often coincide with biogenic Volatile Organic Compounds (bVOC) emissions and contain high O/C from hydroxyl groups in primary plant materials [<a href="#R48" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R48">Medeiros et al., 2006</a>]. Biomass burning factors [<a href="#R16" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R16">Corrigan et al., 2013</a>; <a href="#R28" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R28">Hawkins and Russell, 2010</a>; <a href="#R81" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R81">Takahama et al., 2011</a>] from FTIR PMF also have high non-acid carbonyl group content similar to biogenic factors, possibly from oxidation at higher temperatures in wildfires or lower temperatures in residential burning [<a href="#R16" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R16">Corrigan et al., 2013</a>].</p>
<p id="P6">FTIR biogenic SOA has been characterized by PMF and clustering in several field studies and shows 15 to 25% hydroxyl, 14 to 41% carbonyl, and 14 to 25% carboxylic acid groups in a variety of atmospheric conditions [<a href="#R16" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R16">Corrigan et al., 2013</a>; <a href="#R70" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R70">Schwartz et al., 2010</a>; <a href="#R81" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R81">Takahama et al., 2011</a>] and chamber studies [<a href="#R57" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R57">Palen et al., 1992</a>; <a href="#R68" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R68">Russell et al., 2011</a>; <a href="#R70" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R70">Schwartz et al., 2010</a>]. Organic functional groups of FTIR biogenic factors identified at Whistler, British Colombia, were similar to SOA reported from chamber oxidation of bVOCs [<a href="#R70" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R70">Schwartz et al., 2010</a>].</p>
<p id="P7">To understand the influence of NO<sub>x</sub> and other pollutants on biogenic SOA, we compared measurements from the Southern Oxidant and Aerosol Study (SOAS) 2013 at Look Rock (LRK), Tennessee, and Centreville (CTR), Alabama. The differences and similarities between the aerosol sources and composition at these two sites were evaluated by comparing the AMS and FTIR PMF factor concentrations with anthropogenic source marker concentrations. Comparisons to FTIR results from lab-generated biogenic SOA properties provide the likely source of biogenic SOA. Model simulations were compared at the two sites and used to investigate the regional representativeness of these two sites. Both the measurements and the simulations illustrate how NO<sub>x</sub> affects biogenic SOA composition and concentration in the southeastern U.S.</p></section><section id="S2"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">2. Aerosol Measurements at LRK and CTR</h2>
<p id="P8">Aerosol measurements were made from 1 June 2013 to 18 July 2013 at LRK and CTR in the southeastern U.S. The LRK site (35.63314 N, 83.94185 W) is on the northern slope of the Great Smoky Mountains and has an elevation of 801 m above sea level, where a permanent structure with a long-term IMPROVE sampling program has measured O<sub>3</sub> and PM<sub>2.5</sub> components. The CTR site (32.90289 N, 87.24968 W) is part of the Southeastern Aerosol Research and Characterization (SEARCH) air quality sampling network within a high isoprene-emitting broad-leaf forest and is located on the west side of the Cahaba River at an elevation of 126 m. Both sites are forested and rural. Local times are used in all comparisons to better reflect diurnal patterns in the measurements. Note that in summer LRK uses Eastern Daylight Time zone and CTR uses Central Daylight Time, but time correlations are calculated based on local time at both sites. For example, 4pm EDT at LRK is compared with 4pm CDT at CTR. VOCs are measured at CTR by gas-chromatography mass-spectrometry (GC-MS) as described by <a href="#R89" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R89">Xu et al [2015a]</a>. Following <a href="#R21" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R21">Devore and Berk [2012]</a>, the sample Pearson correlation coefficient (r) is used to discriminate among relationships which exhibit weak (|r|&lt; 0.5), moderate (0.5 &lt; |r|&lt; 0.8), and strong (|r|&gt; 0.8) correlations. |r|&lt;0.35 is identified as “no correlation” [<a href="#R82" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R82">Taylor, 1990</a>].</p>
<section id="S3"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.1. Aerosol Particle Measurements</h3>
<p id="P9">At LRK, a climate-controlled van with an isokinetic inlet pulled air into the van for distribution to a high-resolution time-of-flight aerosol mass spectrometer (AMS), a scanning electrical mobility spectrometer (SEMS), and filters for FTIR and X-ray fluorescence (XRF). At CTR, submicron aerosol particles were collected in a van with air drawn from a window-mounted inlet and through a silica gel denuder with a sharp-cut cyclone for collection of dry PM<sub>1</sub> and PM<sub>2.5</sub> particles on filters.</p>
<p id="P10">Filter samples were collected on pre-scanned Teflon filters (Teflon, Pall Life Science Inc., 37mm diameter, 1.0 μm pore size) at CTR and LRK behind PM<sub>1</sub> and PM<sub>2.5</sub> sharp-cut cyclones (SCC2.229 PM<sub>1</sub> and SCC2.229 PM<sub>2.5</sub>, BGI Inc). Four PM<sub>1</sub> (from 0800 to 1200, from 1200 to 1600, from 1600 to 1900, and from 2000 to 0700 local time) and two PM<sub>2.5</sub> (from 0800 to 1900 and from 2000 to 0700 local time) samples were collected each day at each site. Samples were frozen and transported to the Scripps laboratory for FTIR spectroscopy. A Bruker Tensor 27 FTIR spectrometer with a deuterated triglycine sulfate (DTGS) detector (Bruker, Waltham, MA) was used to scan the filters both before and after sampling. Filters were installed in the sampling van each morning and solenoid valves controlled the start and stop of collection; the filter holders were mounted in a 5 ft<sup>3</sup> refrigerator to keep the filter holders at 4 °C during and after collection each day and minimize losses due to vaporization of higher vapor pressure components, as well as reactions that could change organic composition during storage. An automated algorithm was applied to quantify the mass of the organic functional groups [<a href="#R67" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R67">Russell et al., 2009</a>; <a href="#R80" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R80">Takahama et al., 2013</a>]. Five groups (alkane, amine, hydroxyl, carbonyl and carboxylic acid) were quantified by the area of absorption peaks, and the sum of the mass of the five functional groups is used as the quantified OM [<a href="#R45" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R45">Maria et al., 2002</a>]. Absorption peaks for other groups (organosulfate, organonitrate, aromatic and alkene group) were fit but more than 90% of the samples were below the limit of quantification and are excluded. Fifty-four filters at LRK and 2 at CTR were selected for X-ray fluorescence (XRF) (Chester Labnet, OR) quantification of major elements above 23 amu. The mass of dust was calculated from the XRF metals as described by <a href="#R85" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R85">Usher et al [2003]</a>.</p>
<p id="P11">The high-resolution time-of-flight AMS measured non-refractory particle mass ionized by electron impact after vaporizing at 600–650<strong>°</strong>C surface [<a href="#R20" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R20">DeCarlo et al., 2006</a>]. The AMS operation, calibration, and measurements at LRK and CTR are reported by <a href="#R40" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R40">Liu et al. [2017]</a> and <a href="#R89" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R89">Xu et al. [2015a]</a>. Measurements at both sites were collected in two modes, V-mode with higher m/z resolving power and W-mode with higher mass sensitivity; single particle light scattering (LS) mode was also used at LRK [<a href="#R40" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R40">Liu et al., 2017</a>; <a href="#R89" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R89">Xu et al., 2015a</a>]. V-mode is reported here because of its high signal sensitivity at 5 min time resolution. Aerosol Chemical Speciation Monitor (ACSM) [<a href="#R55" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R55">Ng et al., 2011</a>] was also deployed at LRK and the ACSM scanning rate was set at 200 ms amu<sup>−1</sup> and collected for 30 min intervals [<a href="#R10" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R10">Budisulistiorini et al., 2015</a>]. CE (Collection Efficient) was applied to mass concentration from AMS and ACSM. <a href="#R10" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R10">Budisulistiorini et al. [2015]</a> used a CE value of 0.5 calculated based on Middlebrook et al. At LRK, the AMS CE of 0.80 was calculated by scaling to SEMS mass distribution (after removing refractory components) using density of 1.5 (calculated from matching the modal peak from AMS to that from SEMS). AMS CE-corrected sulfate was correlated to sulfate from XRF Sulfur with R=0.74 and slope of 1.14) [<a href="#R40" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R40">Liu et al., 2017</a>]; at CTR, a composition-dependent CE with a mean value of 0.59 was applied based on the sulfate and ammonium composition [<a href="#R49" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R49">Middlebrook et al., 2012</a>; <a href="#R89" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R89">Xu et al., 2015a</a>].</p>
<p id="P12">Cloud condensation nuclei (CCN) measurements were collected at LRK and CTR. At CTR, the CCN counter was operated in Scanning Flow CCN Analysis mode [<a href="#R51" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R51">Moore and Nenes, 2009</a>], scanning flow rate sinusoidally from 0.2 to 0.9 L min<sup>−1</sup> then back to 0.2 L min<sup>−1</sup> over 2 min to give CCN spectrum between 0.15 and 0.54 % supersaturation [<a href="#R13" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R13">Cerully et al., 2015</a>]. At LRK size-resolved CCN measurements were conducted at 0.20%, 0.37% and 0.58% supersaturation.</p></section><section id="S4"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.2. CMAQ Model</h3>
<p id="P13">CMAQ v5.1 simulations described by <a href="#R61" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R61">Pye et al. [2015</a>; <a href="#R62" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R62">2017</a>] and <a href="#R52" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R52">Murphy et al. [2017]</a> cover the southeastern United States domain for June 2013 at 12 km by 12 km horizontal resolution using meteorology from Advanced Research Weather Research and Forecasting (WRF) model (ARW) version 3.6.1 [<a href="#R61" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R61">Pye et al., 2015</a>; <a href="#R62" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R62">Pye et al., 2017</a>]. The model includes gas-phase chemistry based on SAPRC07tic [<a href="#R88" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R88">Xie et al., 2013</a>] with additional updates for the formation of isoprene-epoxydiol (IEPOX) SOA [<a href="#R61" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R61">Pye et al., 2015</a>; <a href="#R62" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R62">Pye et al., 2017</a>] and semivolatile organic nitrates, primarily from monoterpene reactions with nitrate radicals [<a href="#R61" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R61">Pye et al., 2015</a>] as well as other semivolatile SOA [<a href="#R62" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R62">Pye et al., 2017</a>]. The semivolatile primary organic aerosol (POA) and potential secondary organic aerosol from combustion emissions (pcSOA) introduced by <a href="#R52" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R52">Murphy et al. [2017]</a> were included to better represent anthropogenic OM.</p></section><section id="S5"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.3. FTIR Measurements of Chamber Experiments</h3>
<p id="P14">Chamber biogenic SOA experiments were carried out at CU Boulder using isoprene and <em>α</em>-pinene (<a href="https://cfpub.epa.gov/ncer_abstracts/index.cfm/fuseaction/display.abstractDetail/abstract/9975/report/F" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://cfpub.epa.gov/ncer_abstracts/index.cfm/fuseaction/display.abstractDetail/abstract/9975/report/F</a>). Three different types of oxidation (NO<sub>3</sub> radicals, O<sub>3</sub>, and OH radicals + NO<sub>x</sub>) were investigated in the experiments. Neutral ammonium sulfate particles were used as seed particles in some of the experiments. RH was 50% in all the experiments. NO<sub>3</sub> radicals were formed with N<sub>2</sub>O<sub>5</sub> with mixing ratio from 0.33 to 1 ppm. For OH radicals + NO<sub>x</sub> conditions, oxidants were 10 ppm CH<sub>3</sub>ONO and 10 ppm NO with ultraviolet light. Two FTIR samples were collected on filters in each biogenic SOA formation experiment. The filters were scanned and spectral peaks were integrated following the same procedure used for the ambient samples [<a href="#R67" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R67">Russell et al., 2009</a>; <a href="#R80" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R80">Takahama et al., 2013</a>].</p></section></section><section id="S6"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">3. Results</h2>
<p id="P15">The LRK and CTR sites are influenced by the Bermuda-Azores High in summer, with warm humid air moving northward and northeastward from the Gulf of Mexico into the interior of the continent [<a href="#R17" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R17">Davis et al., 1997</a>]. The aerosol particle concentrations accumulated for periods of several days with low wind and little precipitation before being washed out [<a href="#R40" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R40">Liu et al., 2017</a>]. The precipitation events overlapped 70% of the time at the two sites during SOAS, synchronizing particle removal and thus contributing to the correlation of the time series of concentrations (<a href="#SD1" class="usa-link">Figure S1</a>).</p>
<section id="S7"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.1. Fine and submicron chemical components of aerosols and level of pollutants</h3>
<p id="P16"><a href="#F1" class="usa-link">Figure 1</a> shows the project average AMS PM<sub>1</sub> non-refractory component and FTIR functional group mass concentrations. AMS non-refractory mass had average concentrations of 5.3 ± 3.7 μg m<sup>−3</sup> at LRK and 7.5 ± 4.0 μg m<sup>−3</sup> at CTR. The fractions of non-refractory components were similar with high OM fractions (67% and 67%, at LRK and CTR, respectively) followed by sulfate (23% and 26% at LRK and CTR, respectively), and ammonium (9% and 6% at LRK and CTR, respectively). Nitrate and chloride mass concentrations were below 2% of OM at both sites (<a href="#F1" class="usa-link">Figure 1</a>). For comparison, ACSM OM concentrations were 4.9 ± 3.0 μg m<sup>−3</sup> at LRK [<a href="#R10" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R10">Budisulistiorini et al., 2015</a>] and 5.2 ± 3.0 μg m<sup>−3</sup> at CTR [<a href="#R69" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R69">Saha et al., 2017</a>]. FTIR OM varied from 0.1 to 12 μg m<sup>−3</sup> at LRK and from 0.2 to 12 μg m<sup>−3</sup> at CTR, with average concentrations of 2.7 ± 1.4 μg m<sup>−3</sup> at LRK and 2.7 ± 1.8 at μg m<sup>−3</sup> at CTR. The functional group compositions were similar the two sites with 52% alkane group mass, followed by 17% carboxylic acid, 14% hydroxyl, 12% carbonyl, and 5% amine group mass at LRK. At CTR, the organic functional group mass fractions were less than 4% different from LRK. PM<sub>2.5</sub> OM was correlated to that of PM<sub>1</sub> (r=0.89 and 0.85) but about 15% higher and with similar concentration (2% higher) at LRK and CTR, respectively. PM<sub>2.5</sub> organic functional group concentrations were also very similar at the two sites.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F1"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 1.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=6463306_nihms-997640-f0001.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/c16e/6463306/019e8acaaf3e/nihms-997640-f0001.jpg" loading="lazy" height="664" width="800" alt="Figure 1."></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F1/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p id="P39">Average of (a) FTIR organic functional group concentrations and (b)AMS OM at LRK and CTR. AMS OM is correlated with FTIR OM (r=0.75 and 0.65, respectively). Pie charts show the mass fractions of organic functional groups (FTIR) and non-refractory species (AMS). The bars represent standard deviation of OM concentrations from 1 June 2013 to 18 July 2013.</p></figcaption></figure><p id="P17">AMS and FTIR OM concentration had moderate correlation coefficients between the two sites with r=0.80 at LRK and r=0.68 at CTR (<a href="#SD1" class="usa-link">Figure S2</a>). FTIR OM concentrations were 20% to 40% lower than ACSM and AMS OM at both sites, consistent with the ±20% uncertainty of each measurement, which has been reported in previous studies [<a href="#R3" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R3">Allan et al., 2003a</a>; <a href="#R4" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R4">Allan et al., 2003b</a>; <a href="#R6" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R6">Bahreini et al., 2009</a>; <a href="#R32" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R32">Jimenez et al., 2016</a>; <a href="#R68" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R68">Russell et al., 2011</a>; <a href="#R80" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R80">Takahama et al., 2013</a>]. Much of the AMS uncertainty is associated with correcting ambient measured concentrations by the AMS CE. Since semi-volatile compounds may evaporate from filters contributing to lower concentrations, the difference in OM concentration between FTIR and AMS may suggest that there were more semi-volatile compounds at CTR, since FTIR has been found to be approximately 70% to 80% of AMS OM at urban sites where substantial HOA (Hydrocarbon-like Organic Aerosol) has been shown to be semivolatile in other studies [<a href="#R18" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R18">Day et al., 2010</a>; <a href="#R24" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R24">Gilardoni et al., 2009</a>; <a href="#R39" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R39">Liu et al., 2012</a>]. Losses of semivolatile components at CTR are also consistent with the CIMS measurements of volatilized OM from a separate set of filters collected at SOAS [<a href="#R44" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R44">Lopez-Hilfiker et al., 2016</a>], which detected 50% of the CTR AMS OM.</p>
<p id="P18">OM, sulfate, BC, and CO concentrations as well as particle sizes had weak to moderate correlations (r= 0.47, 0.51, 0.40, 0.51, and 0.46, respectively) of the time series at the two sites, and the concentrations were all lower at LRK than CTR (<a href="#F2" class="usa-link">Figure 2</a> and <a href="#F3" class="usa-link">Figure 3</a>). Average concentrations were 3.6 and 5.0 μg m<sup>−3</sup> for OM, 1.2 and 1.9 μg m<sup>−3</sup> for sulfate (both measured by AMS), 0.23 and 0.26 μg m<sup>−3</sup> for BC, and 115 and 134 ppb for CO at LRK and CTR, respectively. NO<sub>x</sub> and NO<sub>y</sub> showed almost no correlation of the time series at the two sites, with different mixing ratios and diurnal cycles: NO<sub>x</sub> mixing ratio at CTR was 3 to 10 times higher than at LRK during late night and early morning hours (0100~0900) but was roughly the same mixing ratio during the rest of the day (<a href="#F4" class="usa-link">Figure 4</a>). The higher mixing ratio at CTR suggests that CTR has more anthropogenic emissions than LRK during SOAS for two reasons: (1) substantial vehicle NO<sub>x</sub> emissions surrounding CTR and (2) the higher altitude at LRK (801 m) resulted in less transport of short-lived local emissions. Dust was 3% of submicron mass.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F2"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 2.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=6463306_nihms-997640-f0002.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/c16e/6463306/288aa3802057/nihms-997640-f0002.jpg" loading="lazy" height="393" width="800" alt="Figure 2."></a></p>

<figcaption><p id="P40">Scatter plots of (a) AMS OM [<em>μ</em>g m<sup>−3</sup>] with r of 0.47 and slope of 0.37, but plotted with 3 points &gt;15 <em>μ</em>g m<sup>−3</sup>] not shown; sulfate [<em>μ</em>g m<sup>−3</sup>] with r of 0.51 and slope of 0.39, but plotted with 3 points &gt;15 <em>μ</em>g m<sup>−3</sup> not shown; (b) BC [<em>μ</em>g m<sup>−3</sup>] with r of 0.40 and slope of 0.27 but plotted with 19 points &gt;0.6 <em>μ</em>g m<sup>−3</sup> not shown; nitrate [<em>μ</em>g m<sup>−3</sup>] with r of 0.30 and slope of 0.23 at LRK and CTR. (Note that the points not shown in the plots were included in the correlations and slope fits.)</p></figcaption></figure><figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F3"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 3.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=6463306_nihms-997640-f0003.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/c16e/6463306/3c4f93fe7c31/nihms-997640-f0003.jpg" loading="lazy" height="372" width="800" alt="Figure 3."></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F3/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p id="P41">Scatter plots of (a) CO [ppb] with r of 0.51 and slope of 0.38; NO<sub>x</sub> [ppb] with r of 0.08 and slope of 0.03 but with 1 point &gt;6 ppb not shown, and CO and NOx concentrations multiplied by 100; NO<sub>y</sub> [ppb] with r of 0.22 and slope of 0.10, but with 3 points &gt;6 ppb not shown; (b) SMPS and SEMS number mean size of LRK and CTR. (Note that the points not shown in the plots were included in the correlations and slope fits.)</p></figcaption></figure><figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F4"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 4.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=6463306_nihms-997640-f0004.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/c16e/6463306/af0ed4be2f9d/nihms-997640-f0004.jpg" loading="lazy" height="900" width="752" alt="Figure 4."></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F4/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p id="P42">Diurnal plot of (a) NO<sub>x</sub> and (b) O<sub>3</sub> at both sites. Medians, 25th percentiles and 75 percentiles are shown on the figure.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="S8"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.2. AMS PMF Organic Factors</h3>
<p id="P19">The AMS PMF factors that were identified by <a href="#R89" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R89">Xu et al. [2015a]</a> and <a href="#R40" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R40">Liu et al. [2017]</a> at LRK and CTR during SOAS include two pairs of factors that showed almost identical <em>m/z</em> spectra with cosine similarities higher than 0.98: LRK-Factor44 and CTRMO-OOA (with characteristic <em>m/z</em> 44 signal) and LRK-Factor82 and CTR-Isoprene-OA (with <em>m/z</em> 82 signal), (<a href="#SD1" class="usa-link">Table S1</a>). The factor with high m/z 82 was also referred to as 82fac, IEPOX OA, IEPOX-SOA or Isoprene-OA measured in other studies [<a href="#R9" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R9">Budisulistiorini et al., 2013</a>; <a href="#R10" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R10">Budisulistiorini et al., 2015</a>; <a href="#R14" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R14">Chen et al., 2015</a>; <a href="#R19" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R19">de Sa et al., 2017</a>; <a href="#R30" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R30">Hu et al., 2015</a>; <a href="#R64" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R64">Robinson et al., 2011</a>; <a href="#R75" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R75">Slowik et al., 2011</a>; <a href="#R89" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R89">Xu et al., 2015a</a>] as noted in <a href="#R40" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R40">Liu et al. [2017]</a>, and showed moderate to strong (r=0.6 to 0.88) correlations to sulfate: similar to results reported previously [<a href="#R9" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R9">Budisulistiorini et al., 2013</a>; <a href="#R10" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R10">Budisulistiorini et al., 2015</a>; <a href="#R89" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R89">Xu et al., 2015a</a>]. The factor with high m/z 44 was also referred to as LV-OOA in other publications [<a href="#R58" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R58">Presto et al., 2014</a>; <a href="#R94" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R94">Zhang et al., 2011</a>]. Both LRK-Factor44 and CTR-MO-OOA have high contributions of oxygen-containing organic fragments and account for roughly the same fraction of OM (48% at LRK and 39% at CTR). LRK-Factor44 and CTR-MO-OOA have the highest correlation with atmospheric anthropogenic emission tracers (BC, CO, O<sub>3</sub>, NO<sub>y</sub>). For example, LRK-Factor44 was correlated to CO (R=0.61), with O<sub>3</sub> (R=0.68) and CTR-MO-OOA with CO (R=0.62), with O<sub>3</sub> (R=0.49) with 1 hour time resolution. The correlations are more reflective of day-to-day differences. The high m/z 44 level suggested that this factor likely includes substantial contributions from secondary sources. LRK-Factor82 and CTR-Isoprene-OA have been associated with isomeric isoprene epoxydiols (IEPOX) [<a href="#R40" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R40">Liu et al., 2017</a>; <a href="#R89" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R89">Xu et al., 2015a</a>]. This factor contributed 20% to OM at LRK and 18% to OM at CTR. CTR had a nighttime factor (CTR-LO-OOA) and a biomass burning factor (CTR-BBOA) but LRK did not. LRK also had a less oxidized daytime OM identified as LRK-Factor91 but no factor similar to CTR-LO-OOA. The LRK AMS PMF factors have cosine similarity greater than 0.6 and temporal correlation coefficient greater than 0.8 for the ACSM PMF factors identified at LRK, which were identified by <a href="#R10" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R10">Budisulistiorini et al. [2015]</a> (<a href="#SD1" class="usa-link">Table S1</a>). If the smaller, more common and variable peaks of CO<sup>+</sup>, CO <sub>2</sub><sup>+</sup>, H<sub>2</sub>O<sup>+</sup>, and CHO<sup>+</sup> are excluded, the cosine similarity is 0.99 for the m/z spectra of CTR-LOOOA and LRK-Factor91. This indicates that the larger ions at m/z 55, 67, 77, 91, which are more representative of the parent molecules, have consistent relative concentrations that indicate that both factors have contributions from similar bVOCs. Cosine similarity is a measure of angular separation between two non-zero vectors of an inner product space that measures the cosine of the angle between them.</p></section><section id="S9"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.3. FTIR PMF Organic Factors</h3>
<p id="P20">Three factors are identified by PMF from baselined FTIR spectra at both sites by the method of <a href="#R80" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R80">Takahama et al. [2013]</a> as described in <a href="#SD1" class="usa-link">Supplemental Information</a>. More than 85% of the spectra could be reconstructed by the FTIR PMF factors at LRK and more than 87% at CTR. FTIR PMF spectra for PM<sub>1</sub> and PM<sub>2.5</sub> factors are shown in <a href="#F5" class="usa-link">Figure 5</a>. Spectra of PM<sub>1</sub> and PM<sub>2.5</sub> factors at both sites are almost identical (cosine similarity &gt; 0.94). Cosine similarities between each of the three identified FTIR factors ranged from 0.40 to 0.75. The first factor contributed 36% and 41% of the FTIR OM at LRK and CTR, respectively, and had high cosine similarity (0.99 at Bakersfield and 0.98 at Hyytiälä) to FTIR spectra of fossil fuel combustion factors identified at Bakersfield and Hyytiälä [<a href="#R16" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R16">Corrigan et al., 2013</a>; <a href="#R39" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R39">Liu et al., 2012</a>]. Alkane groups made up 70% of the OM of this first factor, followed by hydroxyl (17%) and carboxylic acid (10%) groups. The O/C of the factor is 0.3, indicating a high fraction of hydrocarbon-like organic components. Consequently, the ratio of carboxylic and carbonyl groups to alkane groups is the lowest of the three factors, consistent with factors related to combustion emissions in other studies [<a href="#R68" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R68">Russell et al., 2011</a>]. The O/C of 0.3 is similar to values reported for AMS SV-OOA (O/C=0.37) and higher than those of AMS HOA (O/C=0.06) [<a href="#R94" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R94">Zhang et al., 2011</a>] likely suggesting the factor includes secondary organic products from combustion sources rather than primary emissions. The factor had weak to moderate correlations with CO (r=0.53), NO<sub>y</sub> (r=0.40), and BC (r=0.51) at LRK but had lower correlations with CO (r=0.28), NO<sub>y</sub> (r=0.23), and BC (r=0.23) at CTR (<a href="#SD1" class="usa-link">Table S2</a>). The Fossil Fuel Combustion (FFC) factor time series was correlated more strongly to Ca, Mn and Fe concentrations (r = 0.3 to 0.6) than the other FTIR factors (r = −0.4 to 0.3) at both sites. These metals were found in combustion sources and have served as combustion tracers in previous studies [<a href="#R1" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R1">Agarwal et al., 2015</a>; <a href="#R15" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R15">Cheung et al., 2010</a>; <a href="#R86" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R86">Verma et al., 2010</a>]. The FFC factors at the two sites peaked in the late afternoon (1600–1900, 40% higher than the rest of the day), indicating a photochemical contribution to the OM as seen from 8 to 9 June at both sites (<a href="#SD1" class="usa-link">Figure S3</a>). This factor has accounted for ~40% of OM at these two rural sites, but it is likely not exclusively from fossil fuel combustion sources. A similar factor accounted for less than 10% of OM at a boreal forest site, consistent with the lower man-made emissions at Whistler [<a href="#R81" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R81">Takahama et al., 2011</a>]. This factor is named as FFC factor following the nomenclature of previous studies [<a href="#R16" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R16">Corrigan et al., 2013</a>; <a href="#R81" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R81">Takahama et al., 2011</a>]. Although this FFC factor is affected by combustion sources, the weak to intermediate correlations indicate that combustion is not the exclusive source of this factor.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F5"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 5.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=6463306_nihms-997640-f0005.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/c16e/6463306/d4ce1805e168/nihms-997640-f0005.jpg" loading="lazy" height="610" width="800" alt="Figure 5."></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F5/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p id="P43">FTR PMF factors spectra in this study as well as Whistler 2008[<a href="#R70" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R70">Schwartz et al., 2010</a>], Bakersfield 2010 [<a href="#R42" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R42">Liu et al., 2012</a>], and Hyytiälä 2010[<a href="#R16" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R16">Corrigan et al., 2013</a>]: (a) Factors similar to FFC, with cosine similarity higher than 0.97; (b) Factors similar to MOA, with cosine similarity higher than 0.95; (c) Factors similar to BOA, with cosine similarity higher than 0.94.</p></figcaption></figure><p id="P21">The second factor has substantial alkane group (57%) and also an absorption region at 2600 cm<sup>−1</sup> associated with carboxylic acid groups (23%), making it more oxidized than the FFC factor with an O/C of 0.5. This factor contributed 25% and 27% of FTIR OM at LRK and CTR, respectively. A similar factor was identified in boreal forests in Hyytiälä, Finland [<a href="#R16" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R16">Corrigan et al., 2013</a>], which had a similar spectrum (cosine similarity &gt;0.94, shown in <a href="#F5" class="usa-link">Figure 5</a>) and the highest correlations to sulfate (r=0.64 and 0.38 for LRK and CTR, respectively), CTR-Isoprene-OA (r=0.75), and LRK-Factor82 (r=0.47). This factor had evidence of man-made primary or secondary organic components and is named Mixed Organic Aerosols (MOA) since it has both anthropogenic sulfate and biogenic isoprene-related organic contributions.</p>
<p id="P22">The third factor has high carbonyl group absorption at 1800 cm<sup>−1</sup> and contributed 25% OM at LRK and 19% OM at CTR. The high non-acid carbonyl group concentration could indicate that the particles are from both biomass burning and biogenic emissions (as reported previously [<a href="#R16" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R16">Corrigan et al., 2013</a>; <a href="#R28" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R28">Hawkins and Russell, 2010</a>]<strong>)</strong>. This factor is identified as biogenic organic aerosols (BOA) because it includes primary and secondary sources of particles from plant vapor emissions and decaying plant matter. The BOA factor is similar to factors identified at forested sites [<a href="#R16" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R16">Corrigan et al., 2013</a>; <a href="#R70" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R70">Schwartz et al., 2010</a>], as shown by the similarity of the FTIR spectra in <a href="#F5" class="usa-link">Figure 5</a>, even though monoterpenes account for more of the bVOC than isoprene at these pine forest sites. The coincidence of the emission timing and locations as well as the similarities in organic functional group biogenic SOA composition does not allow separation of isoprene and monoterpene sources. The largest organic functional group fraction is carbonyl groups (29%), which is consistent with the carbonyl group mass fraction reported for pine forest sites [<a href="#R16" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R16">Corrigan et al., 2013</a>; <a href="#R70" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R70">Schwartz et al., 2010</a>; <a href="#R81" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R81">Takahama et al., 2011</a>]. A biomass burning factor [<a href="#R16" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R16">Corrigan et al., 2013</a>; <a href="#R28" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R28">Hawkins and Russell, 2010</a>; <a href="#R81" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R81">Takahama et al., 2011</a>] was not identified by the multi-hour FTIR samples at either CTR or LRK during SOAS, which is consistent with the small and short-duration biomass burning emissions identified by AMS [<a href="#R89" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R89">Xu et al., 2015a</a>]. The small methylene peaks in BOA at both CTR and LRK indicate a very small fraction of BOA could be vegetative detritus. Alkane and hydroxyl groups each contributed approximately 25% of BOA OM. Carboxylic acid and amine group contributions to the BOA factor are lower than 10% and the factor is highly oxidized with O/C of 0.45. The BOA factor time series correlated to Methyl Vinyl Ketone/Methacrolein (MVK/MACR) concentration time series with r=0.66 at LRK, consistent with these intermediate products serving as markers of isoprene [<a href="#R43" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R43">Liu et al., 2013</a>] and pinene [<a href="#R95" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R95">Zhang et al., 2009</a>] biogenic SOA formation. The correlation of BOA with sulfate is weak with correlation coefficient of 0.45 at LRK and 0.28 at CTR (<a href="#SD1" class="usa-link">Table S2</a>), perhaps because the BOA factor does not include the sulfate-enhanced biogenic oxidation products as these may be included in MOA instead.</p>
<p id="P23">In summary, FTIR PMF factors were consistent with AMS factors in how OM was apportioned to sources, despite the differences in apportioning oxidized SOA fragments and groups noted by previous work [<a href="#R16" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R16">Corrigan et al., 2013</a>]. Specifically, the lower time resolution and lack of fragmentation of FTIR tends to associate oxidation products with functional groups that are associated with products from specific classes of precursor molecules, so that primary and secondary components are in the same factor [<a href="#R68" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R68">Russell et al., 2011</a>]. The higher time resolution of AMS often separates POA hydrocarbon fragments that peak in the morning and evening from the SOA fragments produced during afternoon photochemistry. In addition, fragmentation of molecules in the AMS means that many secondary components (such as CO<sub>2</sub><sup>+</sup>) occur in different peaks than the primary molecules that may have similar chemical composition that were emitted from the same source. These differences in resolution and fragmentation mean that primary and secondary components from the same source are separated into different factors in AMS measurements even though FTIR tends to retain products from the same source in a single factor. In addition, the higher time resolution AMS chemical signatures identified CTR-BBOA, which only had several 1- or 2- hour sharp concentration peaks during the campaign [<a href="#R89" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R89">Xu et al., 2015a</a>].</p></section></section><section id="S10"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">4. Discussion</h2>
<p id="P24">The parallel deployment of both FTIR and AMS OM measurement techniques at CTR and LRK during SOAS provided an opportunity to evaluate the similarities and differences of biogenic SOA formation at the different NO<sub>x</sub> conditions at the two sites. The chemical compositions of biogenic SOA are very similar at both sites and are similar to chamber experiments, but the minor differences show an important role for NO<sub>x</sub>.</p>
<section id="S11"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">4.1. Regional Uniformity of Biogenic SOA in the Southeastern U.S.</h3>
<p id="P25">Although the two SOAS sites in this study are approximately 500 km apart, OM, BC, CO, and sulfate concentrations as well as particle size showed weak to moderate correlations of the time series at the two sites (<a href="#F2" class="usa-link">Figure 2</a> and <a href="#F3" class="usa-link">Figure 3</a>). Organic functional groups and submicron non-refractory mass components (nitrate, sulfate, organic, and ammonium) were similar fractions of submicron particle mass (<a href="#F1" class="usa-link">Figure 1</a>). OM was approximately 70% and sulfate was 20% of the non-refractory mass. Oxidized organic functional groups (non-acid carbonyl, hydroxyl and carboxylic acid) accounted for 50% of OM (<a href="#F1" class="usa-link">Figure 1</a>). Despite the similarity in chemical composition and in submicron particle size (<a href="#F2" class="usa-link">Figure 2</a>, <a href="#F3" class="usa-link">Figure 3</a> and <a href="#SD1" class="usa-link">Figure S4</a>), it is not surprising that the CCN/CN had weak positive correlation at both sites at three different supersaturation levels (r=0.22 to 0.37, <a href="#SD1" class="usa-link">Figure S5</a>). The fact that the correlation is lower relative to the correlations to sulfate, size and number could suggest a role for local factors in organic composition and associated particle hygroscopicity, even though the concentrations are controlled to a substantial extent by scavenging of regional rain (<a href="#SD1" class="usa-link">Figure S1</a>). BC and CO are largely driven by precipitation events and regional transport that result in multi-day events that overwhelm the local diurnal cycles (<a href="#SD1" class="usa-link">Figure S6</a>). These similarities indicate that the aerosol particle concentrations at these two rural locations in the southeastern U.S. are controlled both by scavenging of regional rain and by very similar mixtures of precursor emissions and photochemical reaction pathways.</p>
<p id="P26">The three very similar FTIR factors and two nearly identical AMS factors measured at both sites were consistent with biogenic SOA formation from largely the same emissions and reactions. The two isoprene-related factors LRK-Factor82 and CTRIsoprene-OA accounted for approximately 20% of OM and were correlated strongly to sulfate (r&gt;0.75), suggesting heterogeneous sulfate reactions with IEPOX [<a href="#R40" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R40">Liu et al., 2017</a>] were important at both sites. Factors similar to LRK-Factor82 and CTR-Isoprene-OA were also identified by other summertime studies in the Southeastern U.S. [<a href="#R8" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R8">Budisulistiorini et al., 2016</a>; <a href="#R11" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R11">Budisulistiorini et al., 2017</a>; <a href="#R92" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R92">Xu et al., 2015b</a>]. LRK-MOA and CTR-MOA from FTIR contributed approximately 25% of OM at each site and had a weak correlation of CTR-MOA to CTR-Isoprene-OA (r=0.47) and a moderate correlation of LRK-MOA to LRK-Factor91 (r=0.58), indicating that MOA may also have contributions from biogenic emissions [<a href="#R10" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R10">Budisulistiorini et al., 2015</a>; <a href="#R89" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R89">Xu et al., 2015a</a>].</p>
<p id="P27">With NO<sub>x</sub> + OH and O<sub>3</sub> as oxidants, chamber biogenic SOA were very similar to BOA factors at multiple sites (cosine similarities = 0.84 to 0.90), as shown in <a href="#SD1" class="usa-link">Table S3</a>. Most of these chamber and factor spectra (<a href="#F6" class="usa-link">Figure 6</a>) have both acid and non-acid carbonyl groups and similar shapes of peaks, indicating similar mixtures of functional groups. The peak at 3200 cm<sup>−1</sup> shows a high and broad hydroxyl functional group absorption consistent with previous biogenic factors at Whistler [<a href="#R70" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R70">Schwartz et al., 2010</a>]. The ammonium peak reflects seed particles in chamber samples and ambient ammonium in the field samples were removed from spectra. In contrast, low carbonyl and high organonitrate groups measured in the NO<sub>3</sub> oxidation products of <em>α</em>-pinene were not similar to BOA factors (cosine similarities = 0.38 to 0.61). The high degree of similarity of the chamber isoprene and monoterpene biogenic SOA spectra from NO<sub>x</sub> + OH and O<sub>3</sub> may be one reason that the FTIR PMF BOA factors from Whistler, Hyytiala, and the southeastern U.S. are similar even though they have differing amounts of these two bVOCs (<a href="#F6" class="usa-link">Figure 6</a>). The samples from chamber experiments had lower carbonyl group mass concentration compared to the field studies in which biogenic SOA underwent a longer reaction time with lower oxidants compared to chamber. Chamber biogenic SOA had significantly higher organonitrate group fraction (10 to 20% OM) than the ambient BOA, likely because chamber experiments with OH were run in a high NO<sub>x</sub> regime whereas field samples could have had contributions from low NO<sub>x</sub> conditions as well. The high oxidant level also contributes to the differences between ambient and chamber biogenic SOA. The seeded chamber biogenic SOA was less similar, possibly because the neutral ammonium sulfate did not represent ambient seeds well. The ammonium subtraction process might also contribute to the difference. CTR-BOA and LRK-BOA could have had contributions from both NO<sub>x</sub> + OH and O<sub>3</sub> reactions. FFC and MOA are not similar to chamber biogenic SOA, with cosine similarities lower than 0.4. IEPOX biogenic SOA was not formed because the chamber experiments did not include conditions for low-NO<sub>x</sub>, acid-catalyzed chemistry of isoprene oxidation [<a href="#R39" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R39">Lin et al., 2012</a>; <a href="#R77" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R77">Surratt et al., 2010</a>; <a href="#R78" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R78">Surratt et al., 2007</a>].</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F6"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 6.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=6463306_nihms-997640-f0006.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/c16e/6463306/1041d785972b/nihms-997640-f0006.jpg" loading="lazy" height="537" width="800" alt="Figure 6."></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F6/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p id="P44">Comparison of laboratory-generated biogenic SOA from <em>α</em>-pinene and isoprene to BOA factors from the SOAS CTR and LRK sites, Whistler [<a href="#R70" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R70">Schwartz et al., 2010</a>], and Hyytiälä [<a href="#R16" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R16">Corrigan et al., 2013</a>]. Spectra are normalized at 2927 cm<sup>−1</sup>, a common methylene peak that showed up in all spectra. Ammonium absorption was fitted and removed.</p></figcaption></figure><p id="P28">The CMAQ model simulations (<a href="#SD1" class="usa-link">Text S3</a>) also had similar concentrations at the two sites, except for the higher monoterpene-related biogenic SOA at CTR (<a href="#F7" class="usa-link">Figure 7</a>). The model components that tracked biogenic SOA from isoprene (<a href="#SD1" class="usa-link">Text S3</a>) had similar and substantial concentrations at both sites (0.70 <em>μ</em>g m<sup>−3</sup> at LRK and 0.61 <em>μ</em>g m<sup>−3</sup> at CTR), consistent with the substantial contribution to OM from LRK-Factor82 and CTR-Isoprene-OA. The spatial distribution of simulated IEPOX products is more uniform than the NO<sub>x</sub> in the southeastern U.S. (<a href="#F8" class="usa-link">Figure 8</a>). The sulfate simulated by the CMAQ model was prevalent across the region during the month of June (<a href="#F8" class="usa-link">Figure 8</a>), and distribution of isoprene and monoterpene emissions shows spatial uniformity across the region because of the high forest coverage over most of the region [<a href="#R25" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R25">Guenther et al., 2012</a>; A <a href="#R26" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R26">Guenther et al., 2006</a>; <a href="#R47" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R47">McRoberts et al., 2005</a>]. Consistent with this study, simultaneous ACSM and AMS measurements from multiple field campaigns at multiple sites showed that OA is homogeneous in the greater Atlanta area in summer [<a href="#R92" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R92">Xu et al., 2015b</a>].</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F7"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 7.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=6463306_nihms-997640-f0007.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/c16e/6463306/acdef5ee8701/nihms-997640-f0007.jpg" loading="lazy" height="607" width="800" alt="Figure 7."></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F7/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p id="P45">Average concentration of CMAQ modeled species and heat map of correlation coefficients of CMAQ model species to measurements at (a) CTR and (c) LRK. Low 24-hour significance level pairs (P&gt;0.05) are shaded. (b) Concentrations of CMAQ modeled species. Concentrations of measured species at (d) CTR and (e) LRK. Details of the modeled species can be found in <a href="#SD1" class="usa-link">Text S3</a> and <a href="#SD1" class="usa-link">Table S7</a>. Low concentration species (&lt;0.05 μg m<sup>−3</sup>) are excluded in this figure.</p></figcaption></figure><figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F8"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 8.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=6463306_nihms-997640-f0008.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/c16e/6463306/3e9db9e0ef37/nihms-997640-f0008.jpg" loading="lazy" height="652" width="800" alt="Figure 8."></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F8/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p id="P46">Spatial distribution of (a) NO<sub>x</sub>, (b) sulfate, (c) monoterpene organic nitrate biogenic SOA, and (d) IEPOX related biogenic SOA in the Southeastern U.S. from the CMAQ model. “+” marks Centreville and “×” marks Look Rock.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="S12"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">4.2. Differences in NO<sub>x</sub> Reactions with bVOC in the Southeastern U.S.</h3>
<p id="P29">Despite the regional uniformity of particle composition discussed above, there were also important differences between the two sites. Although the two sites are both rural, LRK is more pristine because it is at 801 m altitude and more than 15 km away from cities and highways while CTR is at 126 m altitude and also less than 10 km from the Centreville city, resulting in the higher NO<sub>x</sub> and NO<sub>y</sub> concentrations at CTR than LRK (<a href="#F3" class="usa-link">Figure 3</a>). Aircraft measurements during the campaign showed a consistent NO<sub>x</sub> decrease with increasing altitude in the lowest 2 km of the atmosphere, with the NO<sub>x</sub> concentration approaching zero at 2 km above ground level [<a href="#R83" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R83">Travis et al., 2016</a>]. NO<sub>x</sub> concentration at CTR also had a clear peak from 0200 to 0900 (<a href="#F4" class="usa-link">Figure 4</a>). Similar nighttime NO<sub>x</sub> increases have been observed at both urban [<a href="#R2" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R2">Alghamdi et al., 2014</a>] and forested sites [<a href="#R2" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R2">Alghamdi et al., 2014</a>; <a href="#R72" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R72">Seok et al., 2013</a>]. Limited ventilation of surface NO<sub>x</sub> emissions in the low nighttime boundary layers may contribute to this the diurnal pattern. Since the high concentration of NO<sub>x</sub> coincides with the northerly wind at CTR (<a href="#SD1" class="usa-link">Figure S7</a>), the early morning NO<sub>x</sub> peak at CTR is likely due to transport from vehicle sources from the I-20 morning commute, which is located 30 km north of the site. The resulting NO<sub>x</sub> spatial distribution (<a href="#F8" class="usa-link">Figure 8</a>) is consistent with National Emissions Inventory at Bibb County (<a href="https://www.epa.gov/air-emissions-inventories/national-emissions-inventory-nei" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://www.epa.gov/air-emissions-inventories/national-emissions-inventory-nei</a>), where NO<sub>x</sub> is largely from mobile sources (50%)).</p>
<p id="P30">Since the measured mixing ratio and diurnal cycle of isoprene (~3 ppb) and monoterpenes (&lt;1 ppb) were similar at the two sites [<a href="#R10" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R10">Budisulistiorini et al., 2015</a>; <a href="#R89" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R89">Xu et al., 2015a</a>], the different NO<sub>x</sub> loadings at LRK and CTR were likely the cause of the differences in the OM concentrations and diurnal patterns [<a href="#R40" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R40">Liu et al., 2017</a>; <a href="#R89" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R89">Xu et al., 2015a</a>]. While OM peaked in the early afternoon (1200–2000) at LRK consistently, it peaked both in late afternoon (1800) and at night (0200) at CTR. The nighttime CTR-BOA was 2.5 times higher than the rest of the time while the nighttime CTR-BOA had similar concentration (92%) with the rest of the time. For example, this diurnal trend is obvious from 11 June to 14 June (<a href="#SD1" class="usa-link">Figure S3</a>). These different diurnal patterns of the two FTIR BOA factors from LRK and CTR suggests that they were driven by different oxidation pathways. In addition, the correlation of NO<sub>y</sub> to O<sub>3</sub> is higher at CTR than LRK in afternoons (1200–1600) with r values of 0.55 and 0.17, respectively, suggesting that NO<sub>x</sub> could have contributed to daytime ozone formation at CTR but not at LRK [<a href="#R50" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R50">Milford et al., 1994</a>]. For this reason, NO<sub>x</sub> at CTR may have contributed both directly to biogenic SOA formation and indirectly by increasing O<sub>3</sub>.</p>
<p id="P31">The direct role of NO<sub>x</sub> in contributing to CTR-BOA is evident in its early morning peak similar to that of CTR-LO-OOA [<a href="#R89" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R89">Xu et al., 2015a</a>], which suggests a contribution of dark NO<sub>3</sub> oxidation as well as OH + NO<sub>x</sub> oxidation (RNO<sub>2</sub> + NO) after sunrise [<a href="#R37" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R37">Lee et al., 2016</a>] that is not present at LRK. Further, the two BOA factors were correlated to NO<sub>x</sub> (r=0.41) at CTR but to O<sub>3</sub> (r=0.51) at LRK. NO<sub>x</sub> had little correlation to biogenic SOA formation at LRK (<a href="#SD1" class="usa-link">Figure S8</a>), and there was no nighttime BOA factor (analogous to CTR-LO-OOA). The daytime LRK-Factor91 accounted for 34% of OM and was correlated to nitrate (r=0.65) and radiation at the surface (r=0.83), consistent with NO<sub>x</sub> contributing to oxidants in a photochemically-driven reaction. Chamber measurements of m/z 91 in products of gas phase isoprene oxidation [<a href="#R8" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R8">Budisulistiorini et al., 2016</a>; Krechmer et al., 2015; <a href="#R41" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R41">Liu et al., 2016</a>; Riva et al., 2016] support this type of formation given the measured <em>m/z</em> 91 in LRK-Factor91. Although the factor peaked in daytime with isoprene, we cannot rule out a contribution of monoterpene to this biogenic SOA type [<a href="#R40" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R40">Liu et al., 2017</a>]. This factor was not evident at CTR, indicating its formation may rely on photochemical reactions favored by low NO<sub>x</sub> conditions.</p>
<p id="P32">CTR-LO-OOA and CTR-BOA had weak to moderate correlations to NO<sub>x</sub> (r=0.36 and 0.69, respectively) for NO<sub>x</sub> higher than 1 ppb (<a href="#F9" class="usa-link">Figure 9</a>). LRK-Factor91 and LRKBOA had moderate correlations of the very limited number of measurements for NO<sub>x</sub> &gt;1 ppb (<a href="#SD1" class="usa-link">Table S4</a>). There was no correlation (r&lt;0.2) of NO<sub>x</sub> with CTR-LOOA, LRK-Factor91, CTR-BOA or LRK-BOA for NO<sub>x</sub> mixing ratio below 0.5 ppb (<a href="#SD1" class="usa-link">Table S4</a>). The measurements of CTR-LO-OOA and CTR-BOA for NO<sub>x</sub> lower than 1 ppb were excluded from the linear regression because the variability below 1 ppb made the correlation coefficients low and fitted slopes uncertain (<a href="#SD1" class="usa-link">Table S5</a>). These results suggest that the enhancement of biogenic SOA by NO<sub>x</sub> is only clear for NO<sub>x</sub> concentrations greater than 1 ppb. NO<sub>x</sub>-related enhancement of biogenic SOA formation resulted in 0.5 ± 0.1 μg m<sup>−3</sup> per 1 ppb NO<sub>x</sub> for CTR-LO-OOA and 1.0 ± 0.3 μg m<sup>−3</sup> per 1 ppb NO<sub>x</sub> for CTR-BOA, based on the slopes of the regression lines shown. However, it is appropriate to note that the correlations between NO<sub>x</sub> and CTR-LO-OOA and CTR-BOA were only weak (r=0.36) and moderate (r=0.69) in the high NO<sub>x</sub> regime, which means that even in the high-NOx regime, NO<sub>x</sub> explains only 12% to 47% of the variance of the bSOA factors.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F9"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 9.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=6463306_nihms-997640-f0009.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/c16e/6463306/b6f9116e914a/nihms-997640-f0009.jpg" loading="lazy" height="540" width="800" alt="Figure 9."></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F9/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p id="P47">Scatter plot of (a) CTR-LO-OOA, CTR-BOA; (b) LRK-Factor91 and LRKBOA, and NO<sub>x.</sub></p></figcaption></figure><p id="P33">The 1 ppb cutoff does not indicate a chemical threshold but rather separates a regime in which the NO<sub>x</sub> is sufficiently low that other factors have stronger, or at least comparable, effects on OM concentration. Since these SOAS measurements covered 36 days, variability in wind direction, precipitation, cloudiness, and bVOC emissions also caused day-to-day differences in OM. For example, while the correlation of isoprene to CTR-LO-OOA and CTR-BOA was below r=0.3, monoterpenes were weakly correlated to CTR-LO-OOA when NO<sub>x</sub> was lower than 1 ppb (r~0.4) but had no correlation (r=0.1) when NO<sub>x</sub> was higher than 1 ppb. This moderate correlation of monoterpenes to bSOA at low NO<sub>x</sub> is also consistent with monoterpenes being a large source of bSOA at CTR [<a href="#R93" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R93">Zhang et al., 2018</a>]. The reason for the difference in correlations to monoterpenes above and below 1 ppb NO<sub>x</sub> could be that the monoterpenes are only limiting when NO<sub>x</sub> is low. However, given the limited sampling time above 1ppb NO<sub>x</sub>, further measurements would be needed to support this explanation.</p>
<p id="P34">In the CMAQ simulation, NO<sub>x</sub> and NO<sub>x</sub>-related biogenic SOA was more spatially variable than sulfate-related biogenic SOA in the SOAS study region and was very low (&lt;0.2 <em>μ</em>g m<sup>−3</sup>) in some areas, such as at LRK (<a href="#F7" class="usa-link">Figure 7</a>). The high spatial variation of NO<sub>x</sub>-related biogenic SOA is consistent with the spatial distribution of NO<sub>x</sub> emissions as well as significant contributions from nitrate radical reactions, which tend to occur at night when atmospheric conditions are more stable and reduce transport. Measured NO<sub>x</sub> mixing ratio was moderately correlated to model components from NO<sub>3</sub> oxidation products of monoterpene, namely particle-phase monoterpene-derived organic nitrates (<a href="#SD1" class="usa-link">Text S3</a>) at CTR (r=0.64 to 0.72) but not at LRK (r&lt;0.25) (<a href="#F6" class="usa-link">Figure 6</a>). The mass concentration of NO<sub>3</sub> oxidation products of monoterpene was 2.3 times higher at CTR at 0.28 μg m<sup>−3</sup> compared to 0.12 μg m<sup>−3</sup> at LRK from the CMAQ model simulation.</p></section></section><section id="S13"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">5. Conclusions</h2>
<p id="P35">Fourier Transform Infrared Spectroscopy and Aerosol Mass Spectrometer measurements of submicron mass at LRK, Tennessee, and CTR, Alabama, showed that even though CTR had more NO<sub>x</sub> from emissions from cities and highways while LRK was more pristine because of its higher elevation, OM composition and source apportionment were very similar at these two sites, although biomass burning and nighttime NO<sub>x</sub> contributed to OM at CTR but not at LRK. The time series of CO, sulfate, BC, and OM concentrations at LRK and CTR had weak and moderate correlations of r= 0.51, 0.51, 0.40 and 0.47, respectively. However, NO<sub>x</sub> had a very low correlation (r=0.08) between the sites with nighttime-to-early-morning peaks 3 to 10 times higher at CTR than at LRK.</p>
<p id="P36">The organic functional group and submicron non-refractory component (sulfate, nitrate, ammonium, organic) compositions were very similar at both sites. Three almost identical FTIR PMF factors of both PM<sub>1</sub> and PM<sub>2.5</sub> had nearly identical contributions to OM with ~40% related to fossil fuel combustion (FFC), ~25% related to mixed organic aerosol (MOA), and ~20% associated with biogenic organic aerosol (BOA) sources. BOA was similar to chamber SOA generated from both isoprene and monoterpene precursors for NO<sub>x</sub> + OH and O<sub>3</sub> oxidants with cosine similarity higher than 0.8. LRK Factor82 and CTR-Isoprene-OA factor was associated with sulfate and with isoprene oxidation products such as IEPOX. This isoprene related factor contributed 22% at LRK and 18% at CTR, consistent with summertime observations from several other sites in the southeastern U.S. [<a href="#R8" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R8">Budisulistiorini et al., 2016</a>; <a href="#R11" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R11">Budisulistiorini et al., 2017</a>; <a href="#R92" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R92">Xu et al., 2015b</a>] and despite the differences in NO<sub>x</sub> concentrations.</p>
<p id="P37">The enhancement of biogenic SOA by NO<sub>x</sub> was only evident for NO<sub>x</sub> higher than 1 ppb, which only occurred at CTR during SOAS. NO<sub>x</sub> enhanced biogenic SOA formation by 0.5±0.1 μg m<sup>−3</sup> for CTR-LO-OOA and 1.0±0.3 μg m<sup>−3</sup> for CTR-BOA above 1 ppb additional biogenic OM for each 1 ppb increase of NO<sub>x</sub>. The negligible contribution of NO<sub>x</sub>-enhanced OM at lower than 1 ppb NO<sub>x</sub> at LRK provided the most striking difference between the two sites. Organic mass (OM) had a maximum in the afternoon at both sites but increased again during nighttime only at CTR. The correlation of biogenic SOA species from the CMAQ model simulations also showed NO<sub>x</sub> produced more OM at CTR during late-night-early-morning periods than at LRK, which may be associated with nitrate-radical oxidation pathways at high NO<sub>x</sub>.</p></section><section id="SM1"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Supplementary Material</h2>
<section class="sm xbox font-sm" id="SD1"><div class="caption p"><span>Supp Info</span></div>
<div class="media p" id="d36e1833"><div class="caption">
<a href="/articles/instance/6463306/bin/NIHMS997640-supplement-Supp_Info.pdf" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" class="usa-link">NIHMS997640-supplement-Supp_Info.pdf</a><sup> (6.2MB, pdf) </sup>
</div></div></section></section><section id="floats-group1" class="floats-group"><section class="bt xbox font-sm" id="BX1"><h2 class="obj_head">Key Points:</h2>
<ul id="L2" class="list" style="list-style-type:disc">
<li><p id="P49">Aerosol concentration and composition are largely similar at two different forested sites during summertime in the southeastern U.S.</p></li>
<li><p id="P50">FTIR of ambient biogenic SOA factors are similar to isoprene and monoterpene chamber experiment, supporting NO<sub>x</sub>-related oxidation pathways.</p></li>
<li><p id="P51">NO<sub>x</sub> increases biogenic SOA by 0.5 ± 0.1 μg m<sup>−3</sup> for CTR-LO-OOA and 1.0 ± 0.3 μg m<sup>−3</sup> for CTR-BOA for each ppb NO<sub>x</sub> above 1 ppb at Centreville but not at Look Rock (where NO<sub>x</sub> was usually below 1 ppb).</p></li>
</ul></section></section><section id="S14" class="ack"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Acknowledgements</h2>
